title: "Foreign relations of Australia"

# Foreign relations of Australia



RG Casey House, Canberra, is the headquarters of the [Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Foreign_Affairs_and_Trade_\(Australia\)).

**Foreign relations of Australia** are influenced by its position as a leading trading nation and as a significant donor of humanitarian aid. [Australia](4689264.md)'s foreign policy is guided by a commitment to [multilateralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilateralism) and [regionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regionalism_\(international_relations\)), as well as to build strong bilateral relations with its allies. Key concerns include free trade, terrorism, refugees, economic cooperation with [Asia](689.md) and stability in the [Indo-Pacific](330456.md). Australia is active in the [United Nations](31769.md) and the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). Given its history of starting and supporting important regional and global initiatives, it has been described as a regional middle power par excellence.[^cite_note-1]

It maintains significant ties with [ASEAN](106539.md) and has become steadfastly allied with [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93New_Zealand_relations), through long-standing ties dating back to the 1800s. The country also has a longstanding alliance with the United States of America. Over recent decades Australia has sought to strengthen its relationship with Asian countries, with this becoming the focus of the country's network of diplomatic missions.[^cite_note-2][^cite_note-3][^cite_note-4][^cite_note-5][^cite_note-6][^cite_note-7] In 2021, Australia signed a significant security partnership with the United Kingdom and the United States of America ([AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS)) aimed at upholding security in the Indo-Pacific region. In 2026 Australia signed a treaty with Papua New Guinea, which [Anthony Albanese](556745.md) and [James Marape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Marape) described as "a mutual defense treaty that will enabled unprecedented integration of their defense forces and military personnel" and in response to Chinese diplomatic statements to the effect that the pact should not preclude relations with other states Marape stated that "This is not a treaty that sets up enemies but consolidates friendships and China — we’ve been transparent — we have told them that Australia … has become our security partner of choice and they understand."[^cite_note-8] The Papua New Guinea-Australia Mutual Defense Treaty or [Pukpuk treaty](16331352.md#Pukpuk_Treaty) benefits both states allowing for deeper cooperation, joint training, interoperability and exchange of skills, and jungle warfare training.[^cite_note-9]

## History

Further information: [Diplomatic history of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_history_of_Australia)

### Post-Federation period

The [Department of External Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_External_Affairs_\(1901%E2%80%931916\)) was one of the inaugural departments created upon the [federation of the Australian colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Australia) in 1901, but largely remained an appendage of the [Prime Minister's Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister's_Department_\(Australia\)). Outside of the prime minister, the role of [Australian High Commissioner to the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_High_Commissioner_to_the_United_Kingdom) (established in 1910) remained the most significant conduit for Australian foreign relations, with its significance emphasised by the fact that the first three appointees were former prime ministers. It has been suggested that, for Australia's early governments, foreign policy meant "relations with London on matters of imperial foreign policy on which Australia might have an interest".[^cite_note-lowy-10]

Australia's first prime minister [Edmund Barton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Barton) was in favour of a uniform foreign policy for the British Empire, suggesting Australia could have no "foreign policy of its own" but expected that the British government would defer to the Australian perspective for "regional" imperial policy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBird20083-11] Barton's successor [Alfred Deakin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Deakin) also repeatedly lobbied the British government for greater consultation on imperial foreign policy and suggested the establishment of an imperial department of state to coordinate policy, as part of his broader support for an [Imperial Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Federation).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBird20084-12] Deakin took some of the first steps towards diplomatic independence by dealing directly with the Japanese consul-general, for which he was reminded by the [Colonial Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Office) that it "expected Australia to conduct any dealings with a foreign power through London". He also dealt directly with the U.S. consul in Sydney to engineer the visit of the [Great White Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_White_Fleet) in 1908.[^cite_note-lowy-10] His actions "set a precedent for unilateralism" in foreign policy that was followed by his immediate successors as prime minister, although with a continued reliance on the British diplomatic service and policy-making apparatus and no efforts to develop Australian equivalents.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBird20084-12]

### World War I and 1920s

World War I brought about an increase in direct Australian engagement with governments outside the British Empire, prompted by strategic concerns including the fate of German territories in the Pacific captured by Australian troops during the war. Prime Minister [Billy Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Hughes) visited the United States in 1918 and "in a series of meetings and speeches, called on the US to cooperate with Australia in ensuring postwar security in the Far East".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECuffe202116-13] At the [1919 Paris Peace Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1919_Paris_Peace_Conference), Hughes led an Australian section within the British Empire delegation and co-signed the [Treaty of Versailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles) on behalf of Australia, as with other British [dominions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion). Hughes lobbied powerfully for Australian interests at the conference, including the granting of [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) mandates over the former [German New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_New_Guinea) and [Nauru](21302.md) and opposition to Japan's [Racial Equality Proposal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_Equality_Proposal) to protect the [White Australia policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Australia_policy).[^cite_note-14]

The 1920s marked "the genesis of a distinct Australian foreign policy", largely in response to changing power dynamics and the decline in British influence in the Pacific following the [Washington Naval Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Naval_Conference) of 1922 influence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECuffe20215-15] Australia continued to rely on "the UK and its imperial machinery for diplomatic representation and economic and material security".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECuffe20214-16] Following the [1923 Imperial Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1923_Imperial_Conference), attempts to formulate a uniform imperial foreign policy were largely abandoned in favour of a system of dominion ratification of British decisions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBird20086-17] Governmental interest in foreign policy declined during the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression_in_Australia) as the [Scullin government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Scullin) concentrated on internal economic matters. In 1929, internationalist [Frederic Eggleston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederic_Eggleston) complained to a [Senate committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Senate_committees) that "no parliament which is responsible for its own foreign policy has less discussion on foreign affairs than does the Australian Parliament".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBird20087-18]

While yet to exchange formal diplomatic representatives, Australia made a number of "quasi-diplomatic" appointments in the post-war period, who functioned as official representatives of the Australian government but held no [diplomatic rank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_rank). These included appointment of [Henry Braddon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Braddon) to the United States in 1918 with the title of "commissioner" and the appointment of Clive Voss as "commercial agent" in France in 1919.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchedvin200824-19] In 1921, Australia appointed its first official representative in Asia, with the appointment of Edward S. Little as trade commissioner to China, based in Shanghai.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchedvin200828-20] Senator [Thomas Bakhap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Bakhap) undertook a trade mission to China in 1922 at the instigation of Hughes,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchedvin200831-21] and in the same year Egbert Sheaf was appointed as a trade commissioner to "the East", based in Singapore.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchedvin200834-22] The initial trade commissioner service was partially funded by state governments and ultimately failed due to a lack of support from Prime Minister [Stanley Bruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Bruce) and state premiers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchedvin200836-23]

### 1930s: appeasement and rearmament

The appointment of the [Lyons government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyons_government) in 1932 marked a renewed interest in foreign policy, complemented by the establishment of foreign policy departments at universities and non-governmental advisory bodies like the [Australian Institute of International Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Institute_of_International_Affairs). Prime Minister [Joseph Lyons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Lyons) took a keen interest in foreign relations and exerted significant influence over the government's foreign policy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBird200823-24] He authorised three "Pacific initiatives" as a sign of greater Australian interest in the Asia-Pacific. The first was the [Australian Eastern Mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Eastern_Mission) of 1934, led by deputy prime minister [John Latham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Latham_\(judge\)), which visited seven Asian countries and has been identified as a milestone in the early development of Australian foreign policy.[^cite_note-25] The second initiative was the appointment in 1935 of Australian representatives in China, the Dutch East Indies, Japan, and United States – albeit below the rank of ambassador – where previously Australia's interests had been represented solely by British officials. The third was Lyons's "Pacific Pact" proposal, which envisioned a [non-aggression pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-aggression_pact) between the major powers in the Pacific. Although he championed the pact at the [1937 Imperial Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1937_Imperial_Conference), discussions failed to progress.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBird200825-26] In Bird's opinion, "the Lyons years should thus be seen as a part of the evolution of Australian external policy from dependency towards autonomy \[…] it is perhaps the continuation and acceleration of the process of transition for which Lyons as Prime Minister ought to be best remembered".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBird2008336-27]

### World War II

The first accredited diplomat sent to a foreign country was [Richard Casey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Casey,_Baron_Casey), appointed as the first Minister to the United States in January 1940.[^cite_note-28] This was followed shortly after by the arrival of the first Australian high commissioner to Canada, and by appointments of Ministers to Japan in 1940 and China in 1941. With the entry of Japan into the war in December 1941 and the failure of British forces at Singapore, there was a genuine fear of an imminent Japanese invasion. The only solution was to come under American protection.[^cite_note-29] Ever since that time, United States has been the most important security ally. The close security relationship with the United States was formalized in 1951 by the Australia, New Zealand, United States Security ([ANZUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANZUS)) Treaty which remains the cornerstone of Australian security arrangements.[^cite_note-30]

In parallel with the evolution of the British Empire to the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations), Australia progressively took responsibility for fully managing its foreign relations with other states. Australia concluded an agreement in 1944 with New Zealand dealing with the security, welfare, and advancement of the people of the dependent territories of the Pacific (the ANZAC pact).[^cite_note-31] Australia was one of the founders of the [United Nations](31769.md) (1945) and the [South Pacific Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Pacific_Commission) (1947), and in 1950, it proposed the [Colombo Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colombo_Plan) to assist developing countries in Asia. After the war, Australia played a role in the [Far Eastern Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_Eastern_Commission) in Japan and supported Indonesian independence during that country's revolt against the Dutch (1945–49).[^cite_note-32]

### Cold War

As the [Cold War](325329.md) deepened, Australia aligned itself fully with the Western Powers. In addition to contributing to UN forces in the [Korean War](16772.md) – it was the first country to announce it would do so after the United States – Australia sent troops to assist in putting down the communist revolt in Malaya in 1948–60 and later to combat the Indonesian-supported invasion of [Sarawak](28258.md) in 1963–65.[^cite_note-33] Australia sent troops to repel communism and assist South Vietnamese and American forces in the [Vietnam War](32611.md), in a move that stirred up antiwar activism at home.[^cite_note-34] Australia has been active in the [Five Eyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Eyes) intelligence alliance, and in the Australia – New Zealand – [United Kingdom](31717.md) agreement and the [Five Power Defence Arrangement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Power_Defence_Arrangement)—successive arrangements with Britain and New Zealand to ensure the security of Singapore and [Malaysia](3607937.md).

### Contemporary issues

After the end of the Cold War, Australia remained an important contributor to UN peacekeeping missions and to other multilateral security missions, often in alliance with the United States. Notably, it joined coalition forces in the Persian [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) in 1991, the [War in Afghanistan (2001-2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001-2021\)), the [Iraq War](5043324.md) of 2003–2011 and the [War in Iraq (2013-2017)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Iraq_\(2013-2017\)). In 1999 Australian peace keeping forces intervened in [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Timor) following its referendum to secede from Indonesia. In 2006 Australia sent a contingent of Australian troops to the state to assist in the [2006 East Timor crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_East_Timor_crisis).[^cite_note-35] Australia has also most recently led security assistance, peacekeeping and policing missions elsewhere in its neighbourhood, including in the [Solomon Islands](265083.md), [Papua New Guinea](22943.md) and [Tonga](30158.md).

In the late 20th Century and early 21st Century, a new element in Australia's foreign relations was the growing relationship with the [People's Republic of China](5405.md). After the establishment of diplomatic relations in December 1972, Sino-Australian relations grew rapidly, to a point where China became Australia's main trading partner and extensive official and people-to-people links were well established. In the first 15 years of the 21st Century, Australia maintained privileged relations with both the United States and China. Since 2017, Sino-Australian relations have deteriorated dramatically, as a result of Australian criticism of policies and actions taken under [General Secretary of the Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) [Xi Jinping](2017814.md). This has strongly influenced recent Australian bilateral and multi-lateral engagements such as the Pacific Step-Up with Pacific Island states, the development of comprehensive strategic partnerships with a number of regional states, and the pursuit of alliances directed at countering Chinese predominance in the Indo-Pacific region. Since 2017, existing security arrangements have been augmented by a revived [Quadrilateral Security Dialogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrilateral_Security_Dialogue) involving India, Japan and United States, the 2021 [AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS) security partnership with the United States and United Kingdom and the 2022 Australia-Japan [Reciprocal Access Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reciprocal_Access_Agreement), which provides for closer Australian-Japanese cooperation on defence and humanitarian operations.

## International agencies, treaties, and agreements

### Membership of international organizations and groupings

See also: [Australia and the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_and_the_United_Nations)

One of the drafters of the UN Charter, Australia has given firm support to the United Nations system. Australia held the first Presidency of the [Security Council](31956.md) in 1946 and provided the first military observers under UN auspices a year later, to Indonesia. It has been a member of the [Security Council](31956.md) a further four times, in 1956–57, 1973–74, 1986–87 and 2013–14.[^cite_note-36] It has been regularly elected a member of the [Economic and Social Council](31958.md) most recently for 2020–22, a member of the [United Nations Human Rights Council](635790.md) in 2018–20 and its predecessor the [UN Commission on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Commission_on_Human_Rights) in the 1990s. Australia takes a prominent part in many other UN activities, including peacekeeping, disarmament negotiations, and narcotics control. In September 1999, acting under a UN Security Council mandate, Australia led an international coalition to restore order in East Timor upon Indonesia's withdrawal from that territory. Australia has also been closely engaged in international development cooperation and humanitarian assistance through the [Specialized agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Specialized_agencies), Funds and Programmes and Regional Commissions of the United Nations and major International Financial Institutions, in particular the [World Bank](36752.md), the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), the [Asian Development Bank](2512.md) and the [Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Infrastructure_Investment_Bank).

Australia is a member of the [G20](2206608.md), the [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development) (OECD), and the [APEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Economic_Cooperation) forum. It is active in meetings of the [Commonwealth Heads of Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Heads_of_Government_Meeting), the [Pacific Islands Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islands_Forum) and other Pacific Islands regional organizations and the [Indian Ocean Rim Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_Rim_Association). It has been a leader in the [Cairns Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairns_Group) – countries pressing for agricultural trade reform in the [Uruguay Round](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruguay_Round) of the [General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Agreement_on_Tariffs_and_Trade) (GATT) negotiations. Australia is also a member of [MIKTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIKTA), an informal and diverse middle power partnership between Mexico, Indonesia, South Korea, Turkey and Australia, led by its foreign ministers, which seeks to promote an effective, rules-based global order.

Australia has devoted particular attention in the early 21st century to promoting regional architecture centred around the countries of the [Association of Southeast Asian Nations](106539.md) (ASEAN), to support dialogue on political, security and economic challenges in the Indo-Pacific region. Australia is an active participant in the ASEAN Regional Forum (ARF), and the ASEAN sponsored [East Asia Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asia_Summit). Australia's place at the 2005 inaugural summit was only secured after it agreed to [reverse its policy and accede](https://doi.org/10.1093/chinesejil/jms057) to ASEAN's [Treaty of Amity and Cooperation in Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Amity_and_Cooperation_in_Southeast_Asia). Australia had been reluctant to sign the treaty out of concerns regarding how it would affect Australia's obligation under other treaty arrangements including [ANZUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANZUS).

### Comprehensive strategic partnerships

Comprehensive strategic partnerships are broad, high level relationships which have increasingly been formalized in the Asia-Pacific region under this description since the early 21st century. They are arrangements which, in general, convey a sense of mutual value, strategic alignment and positive intent to further strengthen ties.[^cite_note-39] They establish the framework for an intensified level of engagement across governments and indicate particularly close bilateral relations. Australia has a formal comprehensive strategic partnership with the following countries and multi-national organizations:

- [Philippines](23440.md) (as of 2015)

  [^cite_note-40]

- [Singapore](27318.md) (as of 2016)

  [^cite_note-41]

  &#x20;(Enhanced CSP 2.0 as of 2025)

  [^cite_note-42]

- [Indonesia](14579.md) (as of 2018)

  [^cite_note-43]

- [India](14533.md) (as of 2020)

  [^cite_note-44]

- [Papua New Guinea](22943.md) (as of 2020)

  [^cite_note-45]

- [Malaysia](3607937.md) (as of 2021)

  [^cite_note-46]

- [South Korea](27019.md) (as of 2021)

  [^cite_note-47]

- [ASEAN](106539.md) (as of 2021)

  [^cite_note-48]

- [Vietnam](202354.md) (as of 2024)

  [^cite_note-49]

In 2014, the Australian prime minister and Chinese president agreed to describe the relationship as a "comprehensive strategic partnership", and this helped facilitate an extensive program of engagement. This partnership went into dormancy, particularly from 2020, but is being gradually revived with the improvement of relations since 2023.[^cite_note-50]

## Trade

See also: [Free trade agreements of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade_agreements_of_Australia)



&#x20; Australia

&#x20; Free trade agreements in force

Overall Australia's largest trading partners are the United States, South Korea, Japan, China, and the United Kingdom. Australia currently has bilateral [Free Trade Agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Trade_Agreement) with New Zealand, the United States, Thailand and Singapore as of 2007 and the United Kingdom as of 2021. As well as this, Australia is in the process undertaking studies on Free Trade Agreements with ASEAN, China, Chile, India, Indonesia, and Malaysia.

- [Australia–Chile Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93Chile_Free_Trade_Agreement)

- [Australia–China Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93China_Free_Trade_Agreement)

- [Australia–Korea Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93Korea_Free_Trade_Agreement)

- [Australia–New Zealand Closer Economic Relations Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closer_Economic_Relations)

- [Australia–United States Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93United_States_Free_Trade_Agreement)

- [Australia–United Kingdom Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_Free_Trade_Agreement)

- Australia–Fiji Free Trade Agreement

### Armaments

To bolster its foreign policy, Australia maintains a very well-equipped military. According to [SIPRI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SIPRI), Australia is the eighth largest importer of major weapons in the world. The US supplied 80 per cent of Australia's imports and Spain 15 per cent.[^cite_note-Wezeman-52] All armed services have received new major arms in 2014–18, but mainly aircraft and ships. The F-35 combat aircraft and antisubmarine warfare aircraft from the USA made up 53 per cent of Australian arms imports in 2014–18, while ships from Spain accounted for 29 per cent.[^cite_note-53]

Australia is modernising its armed forces but also acquiring weapons that significantly increase its long-range capabilities. Among the weapons imported in 2010–14 were 5 tanker aircraft and the first of 2 amphibious assault ships from Spain, along with 2 large transport aircraft and 4 airborne early warning (AEW) aircraft from the USA. Australia also received 26 combat aircraft from the US, with 82 more on order (see box 3), as well as 8 anti-submarine warfare aircraft from the US and 3 Hobart destroyers from Spain.

In 2021, after Australia ended its 20-year military mission in Afghanistan, the defence officials held formal talks on strengthening military ties with the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md). However, the human rights groups said that it was "very concerning" to witness, as the Emirates was accused of carrying out "unlawful attacks" in war-torn nations like [Libya](17633.md) and [Yemen](350939.md).[^cite_note-54]

In contrast to 2014–18, the period from 2019 to 2023 saw a 21 per cent decline in Australia's major arms imports.[^cite_note-Wezeman-52] However, in December 2021, Australia signed a defence procurement deal with [South Korea](27019.md) worth \$1billion AUD (US\$720 million) for modern [artillery](2508.md), supply trucks and radars supplied by South Korean defense company [Hanwha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanwha). The South Korean president [Moon Jae-in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Jae-in) and Australian prime minister [Scott Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Morrison) met for the signing of the agreement and additionally announced they were formally upgrading the Australian-South Korean relationship to a "comprehensive strategic partnership".[^cite_note-55] Moreover, in 2023 Australia reached an agreement with the UK and the USA to import at least six nuclear-powered submarines.[^cite_note-Wezeman-52]

## International aid

According to the Australian [think tank](37101.md) [Lowy Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lowy_Institute), Australia is the Pacific region's largest development partner, disbursing A\$17 billion worth of international aid between 2008 and 2021, accounting for 40% of the region's overseas development finance (ODF). Australian international aid in the Pacific exceeded other regional partners including the [Asian Development Bank](2512.md), [China](5405.md), [New Zealand](4913064.md) and [Japan](15573.md). Between 2019 and 2021, Australian's overseas development finance in the Pacific rose from A\$1.34 billion in 2019 to A\$1.89 billion in 2021.[^cite_note-56]

In May 2024, [RNZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNZ) reported that the Albanese Government had slightly increased Australia's foreign aid budget by four percent, bringing its total 2024–2025 aid o A\$4.961 billion budget. In August 2023, the Government had released its new international development strategy, which promised new country, gender, disability and humanitarian aid strategies.[^cite_note-57]

In January 2026, Australia has committed an additional \$50 million in humanitarian aid to Afghanistan, bringing the total aid since 2021 to \$310 million. This support aims to address the worsening crisis exacerbated by Taliban rule, particularly focusing on food security, health services, and aid for women and children.[^cite_note-58]

## Diplomatic relations

As of 2011, Australia had established formal diplomatic relations with all members of the United Nations as well as the [Holy See](13393.md), [Kosovo](17391.md), [Cook Islands](7067.md) and (in 2014) [Niue](21228.md). In many cases, diplomatic relations are maintained on a non-resident basis, with the Australian ambassador or high commissioner based in another country. Since 2012, diplomatic relations have been effectively suspended with the [Syrian Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Arab_Republic), with no diplomatic accreditation by either country maintained, but consular relations continue.[^cite_note-59] In the case of Afghanistan, following the Taliban takeover in 2021, diplomatic relations are in an ambiguous status with Australia "temporarily" closing its embassy in [Kabul](16826.md) and not recognizing the Islamic Emirate government but maintaining the credentials of the embassy of the [Islamic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republic_of_Afghanistan) in Canberra.[^cite_note-60] A number of Canadian missions provide consular assistance to Australians in countries in Africa where Australia does not maintain an office (and Australia reciprocates this arrangement for Canada in some other countries) through the [Canada-Australia Consular Services Sharing Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada-Australia_Consular_Services_Sharing_Agreement).[^cite_note-61]

Due to the [One China Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_China_Policy) of the People's Republic of China, the Australian Office in Taiwan (formerly the Australian Commerce and Industry Office) unofficially represents Australia's interest in [Taiwan](25734.md), serving a function similar to other Australian Consulates.

## Bilateral relations

### Africa

| Country | Formal relations began | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Algeria](358.md) | 8 July 1974[^cite_note-Vol_1974-150] | Australia and Algeria enjoy friendly relations and cooperate in international fora on areas of mutual interest. Algeria has an embassy in [Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canberra). Australia is accredited to Algeria from its embassy in [Paris](22989.md), France. An Australian resident embassy was established in [Algiers](1644.md) in 1975 and was closed in 1991 for budgetary reasons. For a history of Australian representation to Algeria see [List of ambassadors of Australia to Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_Australia_to_Algeria). Bilateral goods and services trade in 2020 was valued at approximately A\$165 million, mostly Australian imports of crude petroleum.[^cite_note-227] |
| [Angola](701.md) | 30 May 1988[^cite_note-228] | Australia and Angola established diplomatic relations in May 1988 with the accreditation by Australia of an ambassador resident in [Lusaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lusaka), Zambia.[^cite_note-austlii_Diplomatic_relations-184] Accreditation soon switched to the high commissioner in [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria), South Africa. Australia also has an honorary consulate in [Luanda](18402.md). Angola has an embassy in Canberra, opened in December 2024. The trade and investment relationship between the two countries is limited, with two way merchandise trade being just over A\$5.6m in 2019–20.[^cite_note-229] |
| [Benin](3459.md) | 11 September 2010[^cite_note-230] | Australia's ambassador is accredited from [Abuja](56823.md), Nigeria. Benin's ambassador is accredited from [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan. |
| [Botswana](3464.md) | 9 July 1973[^cite_note-nla.gov.au-231] | Australia and Botswana have enjoyed good relations since Botswana's independence, as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). The two countries formally established diplomatic relations in July 1973 with the non-resident accreditation of an Australian high commissioner based in [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria). Australia also has an honorary consulate in [Gaborone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaborone). Botswana has a high commission in Canberra and has honorary consulates in Brisbane, Melbourne, Perth, and the New South Wales mid-north coast town of [Kendall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kendall,_New_South_Wales)[^cite_note-232] Governor General [Quentin Bryce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quentin_Bryce) made the first official visit of an Australian governor-general to Botswana in March 2009.[^cite_note-233] President [Ian Khama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Khama) made the first official visit of a Botswanan president to Australia in March 2010. |
| [Burkina Faso](3470.md) | 13 November 2008[^cite_note-234] | Australia and Burkina Faso established diplomatic relations in 2008 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard).[^cite_note-webarchive.nla.gov.au-235] Australia's ambassador is accredited from [Accra](56824.md), Ghana. Burkina Faso's ambassador is accredited from [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan. |
| [Burundi](21490998.md) |  | Australia's ambassador to [Burundi](21490998.md) is accredited from [Nairobi](21482.md), Kenya.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237] Burundi's ambassador is accredited from [Beijing](18603746.md), China, the first presenting credentials in February 2026. It also has an honorary consulate in [Perth](24355.md). |
| [Cape Verde](18962637.md) | 22 September 2009[^cite_note-238] | Australia and Cape Verde established diplomatic relations in 2009 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard).[^cite_note-webarchive.nla.gov.au-235] Australia is accredited to Cape Verde from its embassy in [Lisbon](18091.md), Portugal. Cape Verde has not yet established diplomatic representation to Australia. |
| [Cameroon](5447.md) | 2 March 2002[^cite_note-239] | Australia and Cameroon enjoy good relations as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). The two countries formally established diplomatic relations in March 2002 with the non-resident accreditation of an Australian high commissioner based in [Abuja](56823.md), Nigeria. Australia also maintains an honorary consulate in [Yaoundé](57671.md). The Cameroonian high commissioner is accredited from [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan. Cameroon also maintains an honorary consulate in [Sydney](27862.md). The two countries have modest trade links. In 2019–2020 total merchandise trade was A\$1.3 million.[^cite_note-240] |
| [Central African Republic](5478.md) | 18 January 2010[^cite_note-241] | Australia and the Central African Republic established diplomatic relations in 2010 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard).[^cite_note-webarchive.nla.gov.au-235] Australia is accredited to the Central African Republic from its embassy in [Addis Ababa](52593.md), Ethiopia. The Central African Republic has not yet established diplomatic representation to Australia. |
| [Chad](5488.md) | 2007 | Diplomatic relations were formalized in 2007 with the accreditation of the first Australian ambassador, resident in [Abuja](56823.md), Nigeria. Australia is currently accredited to Chad from its embassy in Paris, France. Chad initially accredited its ambassador resident in [Beijing](18603746.md), China but does not currently have an accreditation to Australia. |
| [Comoros](5403.md) | 27 July 1983[^cite_note-242] | Australia and Comoros established diplomatic relations in July 1983 with the presentation of credentials of Australia's first ambassador. The two countries cooperate as members of the [Indian Ocean Rim Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_Rim_Association) and through small development cooperation projects. Australia's ambassador to Comoros is accredited from [Port Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Louis), Mauritius.[^cite_note-243] Comoros has not yet established diplomatic representation to Australia. |
| [Congo](19599929.md) | 7 May 2009[^cite_note-244] | Australia and Congo established diplomatic relations in 2009 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard).[^cite_note-webarchive.nla.gov.au-235] The Australian ambassador is accredited from [Harare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harare), Zimbabwe. Congo has not yet established diplomatic representation to Australia. |
| [Democratic Republic of the Congo](76762.md) | 2011 | Australia and the DRC established diplomatic relations in 2011 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard). The Australian ambassador is accredited from [Harare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harare), Zimbabwe. The DRC has not yet established diplomatic representation to Australia. Immigration from the DRC to Australia has occurred mainly since the early 2000s. At the end of June 2019, 7,210 people born in the DRC were living in Australia.[^cite_note-245] Trade between the two countries is small. In 2019–2020 total merchandise trade was only A\$3.4 million.[^cite_note-246] |
| [Djibouti](17207794.md) |  | Australia is accredited to Djibouti from its embassy in [Addis Ababa](52593.md), Ethiopia. Djibouti is accredited to Australia from its embassy in [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan. |
| [Egypt](8087628.md) | 8 April 1950, severed diplomatic relations from 6 November 1956 to 19 October 1959 | See [Australia–Egypt relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93Egypt_relations) Egypt was the first Arab country with which Australia established diplomatic relations, with the opening of an Australian legation in [Cairo](6293.md) in 1950. Following President [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser)'s nationalization of the Suez Canal in July 1956, Australian prime minister [Robert Menzies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Menzies) lead a five-nation delegation to Cairo in August 1956 to negotiate with Nasser about the return of the Canal to international control. The Suez Canal was the preeminent economic trade route for Australia at that time.[^cite_note-247] Diplomatic relations were broken by Egypt in November 1956 in protest over subsequent Australian support for the Anglo-French invasion during the [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis).[^cite_note-248] Relations were restored in 1959 and raised to embassy-level in 1961. For a detailed history of Australian representation in Egypt see [List of ambassadors of Australia to Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_Australia_to_Egypt). Egypt has an embassy in Canberra and two consulates-general (in Melbourne and Sydney).[^cite_note-249] There is a significant community in Australia of Egyptian-born immigrants and their descendants, resulting in significant people-to-people ties between the two countries. Immigration from Egypt became significant after the Second World War, including by a large number of Coptic Christians. The 2016 Australian census recorded almost 40,000 Egyptian-born residents.[^cite_note-250] (See also [Egyptian Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Australians)) Australia supported the 1979 [Egypt-Israel peace treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt-Israel_peace_treaty) and has provided deployments of military personnel to the [Multinational Force and Observers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_Force_and_Observers) in the Sinai since 1982. Australia engages with Egypt on a number of shared regional and international priorities, including counter-terrorism and countering violent extremism.[^cite_note-Egypt_Country_Brief-251] in 2016–17 two-way merchandise trade totalled A\$582 million (A\$533 million in exports from Australia and A\$49 million in imports from Egypt). Australia's principal exports to Egypt are agricultural products including vegetables, wool and wheat. Australian companies are prominent investors in Egypt's mining sector.[^cite_note-Egypt_Country_Brief-251] Governor-general [Peter Cosgrove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Cosgrove) visited Egypt in October 2017 to attend commemorations for the 75th anniversary of the Battle of El Alamein and make an official visit that included a meeting with President [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md). This was followed by the visit of Governor General [Sam Mostyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Mostyn) to mark the 75th anniversary of diplomatic relations. Coptic Orthodox Pope [Tawadros II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawadros_II) visited Australia in September 2017.[^cite_note-Egypt_Country_Brief-251] |
| [Equatorial Guinea](9366.md) | 23 July 2009[^cite_note-252] | Australia and Equatorial Guinea established diplomatic relations in 2009 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard).[^cite_note-webarchive.nla.gov.au-235] Australia's ambassador is accredited from [Madrid](41188263.md), Spain. Equatorial Guinea's ambassador is accredited from [Beijing](18603746.md), China. |
| [Eritrea](17238590.md) | 24 November 1993 | Both countries established diplomatic relations on 24 November 1993 when first Eritrea's Ambassador to Australia Mr. Fessehaie Abraham presented his credentials[^cite_note-Eritrea_Update2-205] Australia is accredited to Eritrea from its embassy in [Cairo](6293.md), Egypt. Eritrea is accredited to Australia from its embassy in [Beijing](18603746.md), China and has a consulate-general in [Melbourne](17306237.md). A small but influential Eritrean refugee community in Australia since the 1970s promoted a sympathetic view of Eritrean independence and in 1989 Australia hosted a quasi-official visit of Eritrean rebel leader [Issaias Afewerki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Issaias_Afewerki). The Australian NGO [Fred Hollows Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred_Hollows_Foundation) has been active in Eritrea since the 1980s in supporting the elimination of trachoma and established Africa's only factory making intra-ocular lenses required for cataract operations.[^cite_note-253] Australia established diplomatic relations with Eritrea following its internationally recognised independence in 1993 and implemented a significant aid program. Eritrea opened an embassy in Canberra that year, which remained open until 2013. Political repression in Eritrea, human rights violations and war with neighbouring Ethiopia led to a chill in political relations and Australia has applied Eritrea-related UN sanctions. The 2016 Australian census recorded 4,303 Eritrean-born residents, mostly living in Melbourne.[^cite_note-254] |
| [Eswatini](27451.md) | 9 July 1973[^cite_note-nla.gov.au-231] | Australia and Eswatini have enjoyed good relations since Eswatini's independence, as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). The two countries formally established diplomatic relations in July 1973 with the non-resident accreditation of an Australian high commissioner based in [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria), South Africa. Eswatini is accredited to Australia from its High Commission in [Kuala Lumpur](16854.md), Malaysia. |
| [Ethiopia](187749.md) | 13 December 1965[^cite_note-255] | Ethiopia and Australia have enjoyed diplomatic relations since 1965 with an Australian ambassador accredited on a non-resident basis from [Nairobi](21482.md), Kenya. In 1984 an Australian embassy was opened in [Addis Ababa](52593.md). It was closed in 1987 due to budget cuts and accreditation returned to the High Commission in Nairobi. In September 2010, a resident Ambassador was reappointed and since then Australia has continued to maintain an embassy in [Addis Ababa](52593.md), with concurrent accreditation to the African Union. For a detailed history of Australian representation in Ethiopia see [List of ambassadors of Australia to Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_Australia_to_Ethiopia). Ethiopia opened an embassy in Canberra in December 2013. Prior to this Ethiopia's ambassador was accredited from [Tokyo](30057.md).[^cite_note-256] Emperor [Haile Selassie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haile_Selassie) I paid a 5-day state visit to Australia in May 1968. This was commemorated, ahead of its 50th anniversary, in a 2017 visit by the president of the [Crown Council of Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Council_of_Ethiopia) Prince [Ermias Sahle Selassie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermias_Sahle_Selassie), the Emperor's grandson.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] Australian governor-general [Quentin Bryce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quentin_Bryce) made a state visit to Ethiopia in March 2009.[^cite_note-259] A Memorandum of Understanding on Development Cooperation and a Bilateral Cooperation Agreement were signed by the two countries in May 2012 and January 2013 respectively. Australia has a small but growing Ethiopian-born community, primarily in Melbourne, Victoria. The 2016 Australian census recorded 11,792 Ethiopia-born people of diverse ethnic and linguistic heritage.[^cite_note-260] See also [Ethiopian Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Australians). Merchandise trade between Australia and Ethiopia is small, valued at A\$31 million in 2019–2020 and mainly comprising Australian imports of coffee.[^cite_note-261] |
| [Gabon](12027.md) | 20 October 1984 | Both countries established diplomatic relations on 20 October 1984 when Mr. A. R.Taylor, presented his letter of credence as non-resident Ambassador of Australia to Gabon.[^cite_note-262] At the time Gabon joined the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) in 2022, Australia's accredited representative was redesignated high commissioner. Gabon has not yet accredited a representative to Australia. Two way merchandise trade between the two countries was almost A\$120 million in 2019–2020. A significant part of that trade is Australian imports of crude petroleum and Gabonese imports of aircraft.[^cite_note-263] President [Ali Bongo Ondimba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Bongo_Ondimba) made a state visit to Australia in 2012.[^cite_note-264] His father, President Omar Bongo, had earlier been officially welcomed to Australia during a largely private visit in 1978.[^cite_note-265] |
| [Gambia](65656.md) | 15 February 1982[^cite_note-266] | Australia and The Gambia have enjoyed good relations as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). Diplomatic relations were established upon the presentation of credentials in February 1982 of Australia's first high commissioner, then resident in [Accra](56824.md), Ghana. Australia is currently accredited to The Gambia from its high commission in [Abuja](56823.md), Nigeria. The Gambia is accredited to Australia from its embassy in [Riyadh](51554.md), Saudi Arabia. |
| [Ghana](12067.md) | 6 December 1957 | Australia has a high commission in [Accra](56824.md). For a detailed history of Australian representation in Ghana see [List of high commissioners of Australia to Ghana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_commissioners_of_Australia_to_Ghana). Ghana has a high commission in [Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canberra) and a consulate-general in [Sydney](27862.md).[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268] Australia's trade relations are modest, valued at A\$113 million in 2007, most of that exports to Ghana. Australian mining investment in Ghana has grown in recent years, primarily in the gold mining sector. Australia also provides foreign aid to Ghana to alleviate poverty, improve the environment and promote human rights.[^cite_note-269] |
| [Guinea](12176.md) |  | Australia and Guinea share close interests in the mining sector. They are the two largest bauxite producers in the world (see [List of countries by bauxite production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_bauxite_production)) and were founding signatories in the 1970s of the (now defunct) International Bauxite Agreement. Australian companies are involved in developing the Guinean mining sector. Australia is accredited to Guinea from its high commission in [Accra](56824.md), Ghana. Guinea is accredited to Australia from its embassy in [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan. |
| [Guinea-Bissau](12186.md) |  | Australia granted *de jure* recognition to Guinea-Bissau on 11 August 1974, having extended *de facto* recognition in June 1974 in support of its independence campaign.[^cite_note-270] Australia and Guinea-Bissau established diplomatic relations in March 2011 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard).[^cite_note-271] Australia's ambassador is accredited from [Lisbon](18091.md), Portugal. Guinea-Bissau has not yet established representation to Australia. |
| [Ivory Coast](57744.md) | 17 September 1974[^cite_note-Australian_Representation_Overseas-272] | Australia and Côte d'Ivoire established diplomatic relations with the accreditation in September 1974 of an Australian ambassador resident in [Accra](56824.md), Ghana. Côte d'Ivoire opened an embassy in Canberra in 2016 but this was closed in 2021.[^cite_note-273] Two way merchandise trade between the countries was A\$123 million in 2019–2020.[^cite_note-274] |
| [Kenya](188171.md) | 23 August 1965 | See [Australia–Kenya relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93Kenya_relations) Australia's relations with Kenya are based on Kenya's key role and position in East Africa and its importance in multilateral bodies such as the United Nations, the Commonwealth and the World Trade Organization. Australia has a high commission in [Nairobi](21482.md), opened in September 1965.[^cite_note-Ties_with_Africa-106] For a detailed history of Australian representation in Kenya see [List of high commissioners of Australia to Kenya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_commissioners_of_Australia_to_Kenya). Kenya has a high commission in Canberra, opened in 1983.[^cite_note-275] Australian governor-general [Quentin Bryce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quentin_Bryce) made a state visit to Kenya in March 2009 to promote bilateral relations.[^cite_note-276] There is a small but growing Kenya-born community in Australia. The latest Australian Census in 2016 recorded 17,652 Kenya-born people in Australia.[^cite_note-277] Trade has been growing between the two countries, with two-way merchandise trade reaching \$115 million in 2018–19. Wheat and minerals are Australia's main exports to Kenya, while coffee and vegetables make up the bulk of imports.[^cite_note-278] Australia has provided development assistance to Kenya since the late 20th Century. |
| [Lesotho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesotho) | 9 July 1973[^cite_note-nla.gov.au-231] | Australia and Lesotho have enjoyed good relations since Lesotho's independence, as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). The two countries formally established diplomatic relations in July 1973 with the non-resident accreditation of an Australian high commissioner based in [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria), South Africa. Lesotho is accredited to Australia from its High Commission in [Kuala Lumpur](16854.md). It also has an honorary consulate-general in [Newcastle](52665.md).[^cite_note-279] |
| [Liberia](17791.md) | 26 September 2008[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281] | Australia and Liberia established diplomatic relations in 2008 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard),[^cite_note-webarchive.nla.gov.au-235] with Australia's high commissioner in [Accra](56824.md), Ghana becoming Australia's first Ambassador to Liberia in February 2009.[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283] Liberia has not yet established representation to Australia. |
| [Libya](17633.md) | 2 January 1978, broke off 19 May 1987, Restored in June 2002 | See [Australia–Libya relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93Libya_relations) Australia and Libya announced the establishment of diplomatic relations on 4 January 1978.[^cite_note-284] A Libyan People's Bureau was opened in Canberra and the Australian ambassador to Italy was accredited on a non-resident basis. Concerns about Libyan destabilising activities in the Pacific region, part of a broader sponsorship by [Muammar Gaddafi](53029.md) of such activities around the world, led to the expulsion of the bureau in Canberra in 1987.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] As part of the thawing of relations between Libya and Western nations following Gaddafi's post 9-11 policy u-turns, diplomatic relations were restored in 2002, the Libyan People's Bureau reopened in Canberra and the Australian ambassador in Rome resumed non-resident accreditation. This has remained the case, though with the bureau's name reverting to embassy following the overthrow of Gaddafi. Australia was a major non-military backer of the revolutionaries during the [Libyan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Libyan_Civil_War), sending more humanitarian aid to Libya than any other single country after the United States.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] It was relatively early to recognise the NTC, doing so on 9 June 2011, months before the [capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tripoli_\(2011\)) of [Tripoli](31232.md).[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290] In December 2011, when then Australian Foreign Affairs Minister [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) travelled to Libya to meet with Libyan Prime Minister [Abdurrahim El-Keib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdurrahim_El-Keib). Rudd ceremonially hoisted the [flag of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Australia) at his country's consulate-general in Tripoli and pledged [Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canberra)'s support for efforts to remove unexploded [landmines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmines) in Libya, as well as advice on Libya's planned transition to democratic governance.[^cite_note-291] There is a small Libyan community in Australia, mainly in Victoria. The 2016 Australian census recorded 2,535 Libyan-born people.[^cite_note-292] The Libyan civil war that has followed Gaddafi's overthrow and the lack of a stable political settlement has constrained the development of trade and investment links between the countries. In 2019–20 total merchandise trade was valued at A\$738 million, almost all being imports by Australia of Libyan crude petroleum.[^cite_note-293] |
| [Madagascar](18964.md) | 22 August 1974[^cite_note-294] | Australia and Madagascar have enjoyed friendly relations since Madagascar's independence in 1960. President [Philibert Tsiranana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philibert_Tsiranana) was the first African head of state to make an official visit to Australia in November 1965.[^cite_note-295] Australia and Madagascar established diplomatic relations in 1974 with the accreditation of an Australian ambassador on a non-resident basis. Australia is currently accredited to Madagascar from the high commission in [Port Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Louis), Mauritius and maintains an honorary consulate in [Antananarivo](56597.md). Madagascar is accredited to Australia from its embassy in [Port Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Louis), Mauritius and maintains an honorary consulate-general in Sydney. Two-way merchandise trade in 2019–2020 was valued at a modest A\$40 million.[^cite_note-296] |
| [Malawi](19088.md) | 1 July 1983 | Both countries established diplomatic relations on 1 July 1983 when Mr. I. L. James, the first High Commissioner of Australia to Malawi presented his letters of credentials.[^cite_note-Vol_1983-175] Australia and Malawi have enjoyed good relations as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). Australia's high commissioner is accredited from [Harare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harare), Zimbabwe.[^cite_note-297] Australia also has an honorary consulate in [Lilongwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lilongwe). Malawi's high commissioner is accredited from [Tokyo](30057.md). Malawi also has an honorary consulate in [Melbourne](17306237.md). Australian companies have mining interests in Malawi, including the Kayelekera uranium project.[^cite_note-298] |
| [Mali](19127.md) | 15 March 1984 | Both countries established diplomatic relations on 15 March 1984 when first non-resident Ambassador of Mali to Australia Mr. Boubacar Toure presented his credentials[^cite_note-Vol_1984-180] Australia is accredited to Mali from its high commission in [Accra](56824.md), Ghana. Mali is accredited to Australia from its embassy in [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan. |
| [Mauritania](18778516.md) | 13 December 2001 | Both countries established diplomatic relations on 13 December 2001.[^cite_note-Butterworths-215] Australia's ambassador is accredited from [Paris](22989.md), France. Mauritania's ambassador is accredited from [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan. Australian companies are present in Mauritania's mining sector and two-way trade between the two countries is mainly mining-related, In 2019–2020 Australia imported about A\$100m in iron ore, and exported about A\$8 million, mainly in specialized civil engineering equipment.[^cite_note-299] |
| [Mauritius](19201.md) | 25 September 1970[^cite_note-mauritius.embassy.gov.au-117] | Australia and Mauritius have enjoyed good relations as members of the Commonwealth of Nations since Mauritius's independence. The two countries formally established diplomatic relations in 1970. There are strong economic, education and people-to-people links.[^cite_note-300] Australia has a high commission in [Port Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Louis). For a detailed history of Australian representation in Mauritius see [List of high commissioners of Australia to Mauritius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_commissioners_of_Australia_to_Mauritius). Mauritius has a high commission in Canberra and four Honorary Consuls based in Brisbane, Sydney, Melbourne and Perth. Australian governor-general [Quentin Bryce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quentin_Bryce) made a state visit to Mauritius in March 2009 to promote bilateral relations.[^cite_note-301] |
| [Morocco](19291.md) | 13 July 1976[^cite_note-Diplomatic_relations_with_Morocco-163] | See [Australia–Morocco relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93Morocco_relations) Australia has had an embassy in [Rabat](57400.md), which opened in 2017. For a detailed history of Australian representation in Morocco see [List of ambassadors of Australia to Morocco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_Australia_to_Morocco). Morocco has an embassy in Canberra, which opened in 2005. Two-way trade between Australia and Morocco was valued at over A\$144 million in 2021, with the main imports from Morocco being chemicals and manufactured clothing, and the main Australian exports being meat, fruit and vegetables. Australian Parliamentary Delegations have visited Morocco in 1993, 2011 and 2013. Australia provided five contingents of signals staff to the UN peace-keeping mission in the Western Sahara ([MINURSO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MINURSO)) between September 1991 and May 1994.[^cite_note-302] Australia follows the UN position on Western Sahara as territory yet to be formally decolonized, and has neither recognised Moroccan sovereignty nor the [Sahrawi Arab Democratic Republic](38762388.md). |
| [Mozambique](19301.md) | 1 April 1982[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304] | Diplomatic relations were established in April 1982 when the first Ambassador of Australia to Mozambique with residence in Harare presented his credentials to President [Samora Machel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samora_Machel). Since Mozambique was admitted to the Commonwealth of Nations, the two countries have exchanged High Commissioners. Australia is currently accredited from its High Commission in [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria), South Africa. In addition, Australia has an honorary consulate in [Maputo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maputo). Mozambique is accredited from its embassy in [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan. Mozambique also has an honorary consulate in [Melbourne](17306237.md). Bilateral merchandise trade reached A\$484 million in 2019–20, comprising mostly Australian aluminium exports.[^cite_note-305] Australian governor-general [Quentin Bryce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quentin_Bryce) made a state visit to Mozambique in April 2009 to promote bilateral relations. This was reciprocated by the state visit of President [Armando Guebuza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armando_Guebuza) in March 2013.[^cite_note-306] |
| [Namibia](21292.md) | 1990 | Australia was an active supporter for the peaceful decolonization of Namibia, becoming a member of the United Nations Council on Namibia in 1974 and contributing military engineers to the United Nations Transition Assistance Group in 1989–1990 (see [Australian contribution to UNTAG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_contribution_to_UNTAG)). Australia's high commissioner to Namibia is accredited from [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria) and has an honorary consulate in [Windhoek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windhoek). Namibia has an honorary consulate-general in [Sydney](27862.md). Australian governor-general [Quentin Bryce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quentin_Bryce) made a state visit to Namibia in March 2009 to promote bilateral relations.[^cite_note-307] |
| [Niger](21373.md) | 7 May 2009[^cite_note-308] | Australia and Niger established diplomatic relations in 2009 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard).[^cite_note-webarchive.nla.gov.au-235] Australia is accredited to Niger from [Abuja](56823.md), Nigeria. The first Australian ambassador presented credentials in Niamey on 25 January 2011.[^cite_note-309]) Niger is accredited to Australia through its embassy in [New Delhi](51585.md), India. President Issoufou Mahamadou made the first visit to Australia by a Nigerien president in October 2017, to promote bilateral relations in such areas as mining, energy and dryland agriculture and security and counter-terrorism cooperation.[^cite_note-310] |
| [Nigeria](21383.md) | 1 October 1960 | Australia and Nigeria enjoy good relations as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). Diplomatic relations were established upon Nigeria's independence in 1960 with the appointment of an Australian high commissioner based in the then capital of [Lagos](85232.md). Periodic Nigeria-Australia Senior Officials Talks are held under a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) between the Nigerian Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Australian Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade signed in September 2012. The third such bilaterial discussions were held in [Abuja](56823.md) in February 2020.[^cite_note-311] Australia has a high commission in [Abuja](56823.md) and an honorary consulate in [Lagos](85232.md). For a detailed history of Australian representation in Nigeria see [List of high commissioners of Australia to Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_commissioners_of_Australia_to_Nigeria). Nigeria has a high commission in [Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canberra). There is a small but growing Nigerian-born community in Australia. The 2016 Australian census recorded 8,488 Nigerian-born residents. Migration from Nigeria started with students in the 1960s and nowadays continues through skilled and family migration.[^cite_note-312] See also [Nigerian Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Australians). Total merchandise trade between the countries in 2019–2020 was A\$460 million, a significant element being Australian imports of crude petroleum.[^cite_note-313] Nigeria-Australia Investment Fora, most recently held in Melbourne in 2020, have sought to promote an expansion of Australian investment.[^cite_note-314] |
| [Rwanda](25645.md) | 2007 | Australia and Rwanda enjoy good relations as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). Diplomatic relations were formalized in 2007.[^cite_note-315] Australia's high commissioner is accredited from [Nairobi](21482.md), Kenya. Rwanda's high commissioner is accredited from [Singapore](27318.md). It also has an honorary consulate-general in [Melbourne](17306237.md). The two countries' trading relationship is modest. In 2019–2020 the two-way merchandise trade was A\$1.6 million.[^cite_note-316] Rwanda exports coffee and tea to Australia while it has also attracted Australian companies in mining, agro-inputs and technology to Rwanda. Additionally, a sizable number of Rwandans have benefited from Australian government scholarships to pursue graduate and post-graduate courses in Australia over the years.[^cite_note-317] In 1994–1995 Australia provided two small military medical contingents to support the post-genocide United Nations Assistance Mission to Rwanda (UNAMIR II).[^cite_note-318] |
| [São Tomé and Príncipe](20026306.md) | 8 July 2009[^cite_note-319] | Australia and São Tomé and Príncipe established diplomatic relations in 2009 as part of a broadening of Australia's engagement with Africa under the Governments of [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) and [Julia Gillard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Gillard).[^cite_note-webarchive.nla.gov.au-235] Australia is accredited to São Tomé and Príncipe from its embassy in [Lisbon](18091.md), Portugal.[^cite_note-320] São Tomé and Príncipe is accredited to Australia through its embassy in Lisbon. |
| [Senegal](27278.md) | 26 September 1974[^cite_note-Australian_Representation_Overseas-272] | See [Australia–Senegal relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93Senegal_relations) Australia and Senegal established diplomatic relations with the accreditation in September 1974 of an Australian ambassador resident in [Accra](56824.md), Ghana. Senegal's ambassador to Australia is accredited from [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan. It maintains an honorary consulate-general in [Melbourne](17306237.md). Two way merchandise trade between the countries was A\$110 million in 2019–2020.[^cite_note-321] |
| [Seychelles](27288.md) | 29 June 1976 | Australia and Seychelles have enjoyed good relations, as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) and the [Indian Ocean Rim Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_Rim_Association), dating back prior to Seychelles' independence. Then Prime Minister [James Mancham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Mancham) made an official visit to Australia in April 1976.[^cite_note-322] Diplomatic relations were established on 29 June 1976, Seychelles' independence day.[^cite_note-statehouse_2023-10-162] Australia's high commissioner to Seychelles is resident in [Port Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Louis), Mauritius. Seychelles' high commissioner to Australia is resident in [New Delhi](51585.md), India. There has been a significant Seychellois diaspora living in Australia, traditionally the largest outside Seychelles, although the number of Seychelles-born residents has dropped in recent years. The latest Australian Census in 2016 recorded 2,519 Seychelles-born people in Australia.[^cite_note-323] The diaspora is significantly enlarged by second and third-generations of Seychellois ancestry who maintain links with the island nation. Many Seychelles political and business leaders have studied in Australia. Total merchandise trade between the two countries in 2019–2020 was valued at A\$10.6 million.[^cite_note-324] Australian governor-general [Quentin Bryce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quentin_Bryce) made a state visit to Seychelles in April 2009 to promote bilateral relations. This was reciprocated in August 2011 by the state visit of President [James Michel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Michel).[^cite_note-325] An Australian lawyer heads the Truth, Reconciliation and National Unity Commission (TRNUC) established by the Seychelles National Assembly in 2018. The TRNUC is examining the political, human rights and legal legacy of Seychelles long-time authoritarian ruler [France-Albert René](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France-Albert_Ren%C3%A9).[^cite_note-326] |
| [Sierra Leone](27308.md) | 9 October 1981[^cite_note-327] | Australia and Sierra Leone have enjoyed good relations as members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). Diplomatic relations were formally established in October 1981 with the appointment of the first non-resident Australian high commissioner. Australia is accredited to Sierra Leone from its high commission in [Accra](56824.md), Ghana. Sierra Leone is accredited to Australia from its embassy in [Beijing](18603746.md), China. It also has an honorary consulate-general in [Sydney](27862.md). There is a small Sierra Leone community in Australia, the majority of whom have come after 2001 under the Humanitarian Program. The 2016 Australian census recorded 3,410 Sierra Leone-born persons.[^cite_note-328] Australia has been active in contributing capacity development and small development projects in Sierra Leone, including support to peace building through financial and technical assistance to the UN Peace building Fund and the Special Court for Sierra Leone.[^cite_note-329] Bilateral trade is modest. Total merchandise trade in 2019–2020 was A\$4.5 million.[^cite_note-330] |
| [Somalia](27358.md) | 16 February 1982 | Australia and Somalia first formalized diplomatic relations in February 1982 when a non-resident Somali ambassador presented credentials in Canberra.[^cite_note-331] This subsequently lapsed and the two countries re-established diplomatic relations in 2010.[^cite_note-332] Australia's ambassador to Somalia is accredited from [Nairobi](21482.md), Kenya. Somalia's ambassador to Australia is accredited from [Jakarta](16275.md), Indonesia. The first ambassador since the 1980's presented credentials in February 2026. Australia has supported the state-building and counter-terrorism efforts of the UN Assistance Mission in Somalia (UNSOM) and the African Union Mission in Somalia (AMISOM) and has provided humanitarian assistance. Australia's first involvement was to support the United Nations-approved [Unified Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unified_Task_Force) (UNITAF) which delivered humanitarian aid in 1992–1993. The Australian operation was called [Operation Solace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Solace) and involved the deployment of some 900 military personnel to provide a secure environment for the distribution of humanitarian aid in the Baidoa region. In May 1993, UNITAF handed over to a reinforced UNOSOM II. The Australian Battalion Group withdrew to Australia. The Australian Defence Force Contingent provided movement control, air traffic control, some HQ staff and a ready reaction security team at the Mogadishu airport. The Australian force was withdrawn in 1996.[^cite_note-333] Australia provided over \$61 million to Somalia in 2011 in response to the Horn of Africa humanitarian crisis and has since provided funding to support the AMISOM mission and humanitarian projects.[^cite_note-334] In September 2012 following the election of Hassan Sheikh Ahmed Mohamoud, foreign minister [Bob Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Carr) said," Australia shares the cautious optimism of the international community about Somalia's future prospects."[^cite_note-335] There is a small Somali community in Australia. Most Somalis began to settle in Australia in the early 1990s following the outbreak of the civil war in Somalia. About 80 per cent of the new arrivals came under the aegis of the local Refugee and Special Humanitarian Program. The latest Census in 2016 recorded 7,668 Somalia-born people in Australia, half of whom are resident in [Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_\(Australia\)).[^cite_note-336] See [Somali Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somali_Australians) for more information. Trading ties are modest and mainly comprise Australian merchandise exports. In 2019–2020 this was valued at A\$1.8m[^cite_note-337] |
| [South Africa](17416221.md) | 8 May 1946 | See [Australia–South Africa relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93South_Africa_relations) Australian governor-general Quentin Bryce meets with Nelson Mandela during her stopover in South Africa, 24 March 2009. Australia announced the appointment of its first High Commissioner to South Africa in May 1946, with the high commission being established in Cape Town from August 1946. The Australian High Commission later moved to [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria). For a detailed history of Australian representation in South Africa see [List of high commissioners of Australia to South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_commissioners_of_Australia_to_South_Africa). South Africa established a High Commission in [Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canberra) in 1949. For a detailed history of South African representation in Australia see [List of high commissioners of South Africa to Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_commissioners_of_South_Africa_to_Australia). Both countries are historically linked through the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) and are members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). Following the adoption of the [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid) policy by South Africa in 1948, relations became increasingly strained, particularly from the 1970s onwards, although diplomatic relations were maintained. Relations have been normalized since the inauguration of the first democratically elected Government of South Africa in May 1994, and there are substantial political, economic and people-to-people links between the two countries. In his first year of freedom, [Nelson Mandela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Mandela) visited Australia in 1990 to thank the country for opposing apartheid.[^cite_note-338] According to the 2016 Australian census, 162,450 Australian residents were born in South Africa. South African-born immigrants have distinguished themselves in sport, the arts, media and business – see [South African Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_African_Australians). South Africa is Australia's largest export market in Africa with two-way trade in goods and services totaling A\$3.141 billion in 2021. South Africa is also Australia's most significant investment partner in Africa, with bilateral investment valued at A\$15.2 billion in 2021.[^cite_note-339] Both countries are members of the [Indian Ocean Rim Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_Rim_Association) (IORA) and the [Antarctic Treaty System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antarctic_Treaty_System). |
| [South Sudan](32350676.md) | 24 September 2011 | Foreign Minister Kevin Rudd and his South Sudanese counterpart, Foreign Affairs and International Cooperation Minister Nhial Deng Nhial, sign a joint communique establishing diplomatic relations between Australia and South Sudan. Australia and South Sudan established diplomatic relations on 24 September 2011.[^cite_note-reliefweb.int-225] Australia's ambassador is accredited on a non-resident basis from [Addis Ababa](52593.md), Ethiopia. South Sudan's ambassador is accredited on a non-resident basis from [Beijing](18603746.md). Australia is home to a significant South Sudanese community. According to the 2016 Australian Housing and Population Census, 7,699 Australian residents were born in South Sudan. Community leaders estimate that, counting persons born when South Sudan was part of the Republic of Sudan, and who report their birthplace as Sudan, the total is about 20,000. The majority of the South Sudan-born population arrived in Australia between 2001 and 2006 under Australia's refugee resettlement program.[^cite_note-340] [South Sudanese Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sudanese_Australians) have distinguished themselves in a wide variety of fields including sport, fashion, music and law. Australia has contributed almost \$113 million in humanitarian assistance to South Sudan since the outbreak of conflict in December 2013, including over \$60 million since 2017.[^cite_note-341] Australia has a contingent of Australian Defence Force personnel in South Sudan who have been deployed with the [United Nations Mission in South Sudan](33447906.md) (UNMISS) since its inception in 2011. |
| [Sudan](27421.md) | 8 February 1974[^cite_note-342] | Australia is accredited to Sudan from its embassy in [Cairo](6293.md), Egypt. Sudan has an embassy in [Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canberra), which took over the old Eritrean embassy and opened in 2017. Although Australia had been critical of human rights violations and political repression in Sudan, and has applied Sudan-related UN sanctions, relations have improved since 2017. In September 2018, Australia and Sudan held inaugural Senior Officials Talks in Canberra. The 2016 Australian Census recorded 17,031 Sudan-born people in Australia. Since 2001 many immigrants from Sudan, or those born to Sudanese parents in regugee camps, have come under the Humanitarian Program.[^cite_note-343] Trade between the two countries is modest, totalling about A\$10m in 2019–2020.[^cite_note-344] Sudan regularly attends the Africa Down Under and the International Mining and Resources Conferences at ministerial level. |
| [Tanzania](30118.md) | 11 May 1962 | Australia announced the establishment of diplomatic relations with Tanganyika on 11 May 1962.
